as in the statement of the lemma.
We first use regularity to show that there are many edges in the deterministic graph.

Claim
.

We have

\left|\tr_{u}(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})\cap\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})\right|\geq\frac{d^{5}n^{2}}{2},

for all but at most
2\varepsilon n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
.

Proof of Claim
.

We will prove the claim in the case that
\ell=3
, the other cases are identical. For
i\in[2]
, let
X_{i}=N_{\Gamma}\big(u;V_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{i}\big)
and for
v\in V^{3}\setminus\{u\}
, let
Y_{i}(v)=N_{\Gamma}\big(u,v;V_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{i}\big)\subseteq X_{i}
. Since
\Gamma
is
(\varepsilon,d)
-super-regular, we have
|X_{i}|\geq(d-2\varepsilon)n
for both
i\in[2]
(we need the factor of
2
in front of the
\varepsilon
here to take account of the fact that we are potentially missing a vertex in
\underline{u}
).
For
i\in[2]
, let
R_{i}\subset V^{3}
be the set of vertices
v\in V^{3}
for which
|Y_{i}(v)|<(d-2\varepsilon)^{2}n
and let
R=R_{1}\cup R_{2}
.
It follows from the
\varepsilon
-regularity of
(V^{i},V^{3})
and
Lemma

2.8
, that
|R_{i}|\leq\varepsilon n
for both
i\in[2]
and hence
|R|\leq 2\varepsilon n
.
Furthermore, for every
v\in V^{3}\setminus R
, it follows from the
\varepsilon
-regularity of the pair
(V^{2},V^{3})
that

\left|E(\Gamma)\cap(Y_{1}(v)\cup Y_{2}(v))\right|\geq(d-2\varepsilon)^{5}n^{2}
. This completes the proof by our choice of
\varepsilon
.

∎

Observe now that each edge in
E({\Gamma})\cap N_{\Gamma_{\hat{\underline{u}}}}(u,v)=\tr_{u}(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})\cap\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})
is present independently in
\Gamma_{p}
and hence it follows from Chernoff’s inequality (
2.2
) that for all vertices
v
satisfying the conclusion of the claim, we have that

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\tr_{u}(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})\cap\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})\cap E(\Gamma_{p})\right|<\frac{d^{5}pn^{2}}{4}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{d^{5}pn^{2}}{16}\right)\leq e^{-2n},

for sufficiently large
n
. This completes the proof after a union bound over choices of
v\in V^{\ell}
.
∎

5
Embedding (partial) triangle factors

In this section we will prove
3.2
and reduce
3.3
to
Lemma

3.4
. As we have already shown in
Section

3
that
3.1
follows from
3.2
and
3.3
, after this section the only tool used in the proof of

3.1
that still needs to be established is
Lemma

3.4
.

5.1
Counting almost triangle factors

Here we prove
3.2
.

Proof of
3.2
.

Choose
\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{C}\ll\varepsilon^{\prime}\ll\eta,d
and fix some
\Gamma
and
p
as in the statement of the proposition.
By
Lemma

4.1
, we have whp that

\left|K_{3}(\Gamma_{p}[X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup X_{3}])\right|=(pd)^{3}|X_{1}||X_{2}||X_{3}|\pm\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{3}n^{3},

(5.1)

for all
X_{1}\subseteq V^{1}
,
X_{2}\subseteq V^{2}
and
X_{3}\subseteq V^{3}
. We will show by induction on
t
that if
\Gamma_{p}
satisfies
Eq.

5.1
, then it satisfies

\left|\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq(1-\eta)^{t}(pd)^{3t}\left(n!_{t}\right)^{3},

(5.2)

for all integers
t\leq(1-\eta)n
, as claimed.
Firstly, note that
Eq.

5.2
is trivial for
t=0
, recalling that by definition
n!_{0}=1
.
Suppose now
Eq.

5.2
holds for some integer
0\leq t\leq(1-\eta)n
. Fix some
\psi\in\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
and let
X_{i}\subseteq V^{i}
,
i\in[3]
, be the sets of vertices which are not in
\psi(D_{t})
. Note that
\left|X_{i}\right|=n-t
for all
i\in[3]
.
Now the number of triangles which extend
\psi
to an embedding in
\Psi^{t+1}(\Gamma_{p})
is precisely
\left|K_{3}(\Gamma_{p}[X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup X_{3}])\right|
and by (
5.1
), we have

\displaystyle\left|K_{3}(\Gamma_{p}[X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup X_{3}])\right|

\displaystyle\geq(pd)^{3}|X_{1}||X_{2}||X_{3}|-\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{3}n^{3}

\displaystyle\geq(pd)^{3}(n-t)^{3}-\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\eta^{3}d^{3}}(pd)^{3}(n-t)^{3}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\eta)(pd)^{3}(n-t)^{3},

by our choice of constants.
It follows from the induction hypothesis that

\displaystyle\left|\Psi^{t+1}(\Gamma_{p})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|(1-\eta)(pd)^{3}(n-t)^{3}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\eta)^{t+1}(pd)^{3(t+1)}\left(n!_{t+1}\right)^{3},

finishing the proof.
∎

5.2
Extending almost triangle factors

In this subsection, we will prove
3.3
using the Local Distribution Lemma (see
Lemma

3.4
) as a black box for now. We first reduce
3.3
to the following lemma, which concentrates on adding a triangle at a fixed vertex. Recall that given
G\subseteq\Gamma
, a vertex
v\in V^{1}
and some
t\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\Psi_{v}^{t}(G)\subseteq\Psi^{t}(G)
the set of embeddings
\psi\in\Psi^{t}(G)
for which
\psi((1,1))=v
.

Lemma 5.1

(adding a triangle at a fixed vertex)
.

For all
0<d\leq 1
there exists
\alpha,\eta,\varepsilon>0
and
C>0
such that for all sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
p\geq C(\log n)^{1/3}n^{-2/3}
, if
\,\Gamma
is an
(\varepsilon,d)
-super-regular tripartite graph with parts of size
n
, then whp the following holds in
\Gamma_{p}
for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
and for all
v\in V^{1}
.
If

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\left(1-\eta\right)^{n}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2},

then

\left|\Psi_{v}^{t+1}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\alpha(pd)^{3}(n-t)^{2}\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|.

We first show how
3.3
follows from this.

Proof of
3.3
.

Choose
0<\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{C}\ll\eta\ll\eta^{\prime}\ll\alpha\ll\alpha^{\prime}\ll d
. Now by our choice of constants (also choosing
K\geq 5
) and taking a union bound over all choices of
t
with
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
,
\ell\in[3]
and
\underline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
we have whp that the conclusion of
Lemma

3.4
holds in
\Gamma_{p}
for all such choices and also the conclusion of
Lemma

5.1
holds with
\eta^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}
replacing
\eta
and
\alpha
. We will now show that given these conclusions hold in
\Gamma_{p}
, we have the desired statement of
3.3
. So fix some
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
and suppose that

\left|\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\left(1-\eta\right)^{n}(pd)^{3t}(n!_{t})^{3}.

Let
U_{1}\subseteq V^{1}
be the set of vertices
u_{1}\in V^{1}
for which

\left|\Psi_{\hat{u}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{n-t}{n}\right)\left|\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|.

(5.3)

It follows from (the assumed conclusion of)
Lemma

3.4
(with
\ell=1
) that we have
|U_{1}|\geq\tfrac{n}{2}
.
Now as

\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{n-t}{n}\right)\left|\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\left(1-\eta\right)^{n}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2},

and
\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\left(1-\eta\right)^{n}\geq(1-\eta^{\prime})^{n}
,
we have that

\left|\Psi_{u_{1}}^{t+1}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\alpha^{\prime}\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}(pd)^{3}\frac{(n-t)^{3}}{n}\left|\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|,

for every
u_{1}\in U_{1}
, from (the assumed conclusion of)
Lemma

5.1
and
Eq.

5.3
.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\Psi^{t+1}(G)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{u_{1}\in U_{1}}\left|\Psi_{u_{1}}^{t+1}(\Gamma_{p})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}(pd)^{3}(n-t)^{3}\left|\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\alpha(pd)^{3}(n-t)^{3}\left|\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|,

by our choice of constants. This finishes the proof.
∎

It remains to prove
Lemma

5.1
. Before embarking on this, we sketch some of the key ideas involved. For this discussion, we fix some
t\in[n]
and
v\in V^{1}
that we think of as satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

5.1
(including the “if” statement). We will say that a pair
(w_{2},w_{3})\in V^{2}\times V^{3}
is
good
if

\left|\Psi_{\hat{\underline{w}}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|=\Omega\left(\left(\frac{n-t}{n}\right)^{2}\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\right),

where
\underline{w}=(v,w_{2},w_{3})
. Note that we can appeal to the Local Distribution Lemma (
Lemma

3.4
) twice (once with
\ell=2
and once with
\ell=3
) to conclude that almost all pairs
(w_{2},w_{3})\in V^{2}\times V^{3}
are good. That is, for almost all choices of
(w_{2},w_{3})\in V^{2}\times V^{3}
, we have that there are roughly the ‘correct’ number of embeddings of
D_{t}
that avoid
\underline{w}=(v,w_{2},w_{3})
. Moreover, due to
\Gamma
being super-regular, there will be some proportion of these
(w_{2},w_{3})
(say, at least
\tfrac{1}{2}d^{3}n^{2}
) that form triangles with
v
in
\Gamma
. So we have some set
W\subset V^{2}\times V^{3}
of size at least
\tfrac{1}{2}d^{3}n^{2}
such that all
(w_{2},w_{3})\in W
are good and have that
\{v,w_{2},w_{3}\}\in K_{3}(\Gamma)
. The conclusion of
Lemma

5.1
will then follow if we can prove that at least, say,
\tfrac{p^{3}}{2}|W|
triangles
\{v,w_{2},w_{3}\}
with
(w_{2},w_{3})\in W
, appear in
\Gamma_{p}
. Of course, every triangle in
\Gamma
appears in
\Gamma_{p}
with probability
p^{3}
and so this is something we can expect to be true but we cannot appeal to standard tools to prove this.

The issue here is that
W
itself is a random set as the property of
being good
depends on the random edges that appear in
\Gamma_{p}
. Indeed, in order to determine whether an edge
(w_{2},w_{3})\in V^{2}\times V^{3}
is good or not, we need to count the number of embeddings of
D_{t}
in
\Gamma_{p}
that avoid
\underline{w}=(v,w_{2},w_{3})
and so certainly need to know the random status of edges in
\Gamma_{p}
to carry out this count. However, crucially,
W
does not depend on
all
the random edges. Indeed, for
any

(w_{2},w_{3})\in V^{2}\times V^{3}
, we can determine whether
(w_{2},w_{3})
is in our set
W
without knowing the random status of edges adjacent to
v
. Indeed, as the property of being good only depends on counting embeddings that
avoid

v
, the random status of edges adjacent to
v
has no bearing on whether an edge
(w_{2},w_{3})\in V^{2}\times V^{3}
is good or not. Therefore, by appealing to a two-stage revealing process (see
Lemma

4.6
Item

(ii)
), we will be able to prove
Lemma

5.1
if we know that at least, say,
\tfrac{p}{2}|W|
of the pairs
(w_{2},w_{3})\in W
host edges in
\Gamma_{p}
, as then we will be able to conclude that roughly a
{p^{2}}
proportion of these edges in
W\cap E(\Gamma_{p})
extend to triangles with
v
in
\Gamma_{p}
.

Again, requiring that
\tfrac{p}{2}|W|
edges in
W
appear in
\Gamma_{p}
is certainly a natural thing to expect as each edge appears with probability
p
, but again the set
W
containing good edges, depends heavily on the random status of edges in
\Gamma[V^{2},V^{3}]
. Our aim is to use a two-stage revealing process, manipulating independence, as above. Again here, it is crucial that we are counting embeddings that avoid vertices. That is, if
e=\{w_{2},w_{3}\}\in E(\Gamma[V^{2},V^{3}])
, then in order to determine the number of embeddings that avoid
(v,w_{2},w_{3})
, we do not need to know the random status of
e
and in fact more is true. The number of embeddings of
D_{t}
avoiding
(v,w_{2},w_{3})
is independent of the random status of
all

(w_{2},u_{3})
with
u_{3}\in N_{\Gamma}(w_{2};V^{3})
. Therefore our approach is to lower bound the number of edges in
|W\cap\Gamma_{p}|
by grouping together edges in
W
according to their
V^{2}
-endpoint. This gives hope to use a two-stage random revealing argument (appealing to
Lemma

4.6
Item

(i)
) to conclude that roughly the expected number of good edges appear in
\Gamma_{p}
.

However, there is an oversight in the discussion above. The point is that our definition of whether an edge
(w_{2},w_{3})\in V^{2}\times V^{3}
is good does not only rely on counting embeddings avoiding
\underline{w}=(v,w_{2},w_{3})
, we
also
need to know the size of
\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|
. Therefore, if
e=\{w_{2},w_{3}\}\in E(\Gamma[V^{2},V^{3}])
, then in order to determine if
(w_{2},w_{3})
is
good
, we actually need to reveal the random status of
e
itself as well as all the random edges between
V^{2}
and
V^{3}
(to determine
\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|
). To remedy this, we adjust our definition of good to be independent of
\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|
. We will therefore give a
grading
of the possible range of
\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|
and show that the desired conclusion holds with respect to each
grade
(see
5.3
in the proof). In order to be able to perform a union bound over all of the possible grades, we need an upper bound on how large
\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|
can be (whp) and this is provided by
5.2
. This idea allows us to remove
\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|
from the definition of being good, leading to the definition of being
sound
in the proof. Hence we have that for
e=\{w_{2},w_{3}\}\in\Gamma[V^{2},V^{3}]
, whether
(w_{2},w_{3})
is sound or not relies only on counting embeddings avoiding
\underline{w}=(v,w_{2},w_{3})
and so is independent of whether
e
appears in
\Gamma_{p}
and in fact, as sketched above, the ‘soundness’ of
(w_{2},w_{3})
is independent of the random status of
all

(w_{2},u_{3})
with
u_{3}\in N_{\Gamma}(w_{2};V^{3})
. We therefore consider potential triangles one vertex at a time and we refine our definition of sound to handle this, leading to the definition of
sound tuples
in the proof. We now give the full details of the proof of
Lemma

5.1
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.1
.

Choose
K=L=10
and
0\ll\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{C}\ll\eta\ll\eta^{\prime}\ll\alpha\ll d,\frac{1}{K},\frac{1}{L}
and fix
p
and
\Gamma
as in the statement of the lemma. We begin by showing the following simple claim which gives a weak upper bound on the number of embeddings that avoid a fixed vertex
v_{1}
.

Claim 5.2
.

We have that the following statement holds whp in
\Gamma_{p}
. For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
and
v_{1}\in V^{1}
, we have that

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\leq n^{3}p^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2}.

(5.4)

Proof of Claim
.

Fix some
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
v_{1}\in V^{1}
as in the statement of the claim. Then

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma)\right|\leq\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(K_{n,n,n})\right|\leq(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2},

and so, as each embedding of
D_{t}
in
\Gamma
appears in
\Gamma_{p}
with probability
p^{3t}
, we have that
\lambda:=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\right]\leq p^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2}
. Therefore, appealing to Markov’s inequality gives that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|>n^{3}p^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|>n^{3}\lambda\right]\leq\frac{1}{n^{3}}.

Taking a union bound over the choices of
v\in V^{1}
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
(1-\eta)n\leq t\leq n
completes the proof of the claim.

∎

5.2
gives us an upper bound on the size of
\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
that holds whp, whilst the statement of the lemma gives a lower bound. Our next claim replaces the lower bound in the statement of the lemma, with lower bounds independent of
\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|
. These lower bounds will depend on a parameter
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and we make the following definitions which will define the range of
s
we are interested in. Firstly let
s_{0}
be the largest (negative)
s\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
2^{s}\leq(1-\eta)^{n}
. Further, let
s_{1}
be the minimum integer
s\in\mathbb{N}
such that
2^{s}d^{3t}\geq n^{3}
. So we have that

s_{0}\geq n\frac{\log\left(1-\eta\right)}{\log 2}-1\geq-n\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad s_{1}\leq\frac{3\log n-3t\log(d)}{\log 2}+1\leq\frac{n}{\alpha}.

Finally, let
\mathds{S}:=\{s\in\mathbb{Z}:s_{0}\leq s\leq s_{1}\}
. We now state our second claim.

Claim 5.3
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
,
s\in\mathds{S}
and
v_{1}\in V^{1}
, with probability at least
1-n^{-4}
, the following statement holds in
\Gamma_{p}
.
If

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq 2^{s}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2},

(5.5)

then

\left|\Psi_{v_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq 2^{s+1}\alpha(pd)^{3(t+1)}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t+1})^{2}.

Before proving
5.3
, we show how the lemma follows from the two claims. Taking a union bound, we can conclude that whp the conclusion of
5.3
holds for all choices of
t,s
and
v_{1}
(noting that
|\mathds{S}|\leq(1+\alpha^{-1})n
), as well as the conclusion of
5.2
. Now suppose that this is the case and let
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
and
v\in V^{1}
. If, as in the assumption of
Lemma

5.1
, we have

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\left(1-\eta\right)^{n}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2},

(5.6)

then, letting
s^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}
be the maximum integer
s\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq 2^{s}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2},

we conclude
from
Eq.

5.6
that
s^{*}\geq s_{0}
and
from (the assumed conclusion of)
5.2
that
s^{*}\leq s_{1}
and hence
s^{*}\in\mathds{S}
. Therefore, from (the assumed conclusion of)
5.3
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{v}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq 2^{s^{*}+1}\alpha(pd)^{3(t+1)}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t+1})^{2}\geq\alpha(pd)^{3}(n-t)^{2}\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|,

as required for the conclusion of
Lemma

5.1
, where we used that

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\leq 2^{s^{*}+1}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2}\,,

by the definition of
s^{*}
. Thus it remains to prove
5.3
.

Proof of
5.3
.

Let us fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
,
s\in\mathds{S}
and
v_{1}\in V^{1}
.
Given some
\ell\in[3]
, we call a sequence of vertices
\underline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell})\in\mathcal{V}

sound
if

\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\left(8\sqrt{\alpha}\right)^{\ell-1}2^{s}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{\ell}(n!_{t})^{3-\ell}.

Note that (
5.5
) holds if and only if
(v_{1})
is sound.
Also note that for any
\underline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell})
and
i\in[\ell]
, we can determine whether
\underline{u}
is sound or not without knowing the random status of edges adjacent to
u_{i}
in
\Gamma
, as determining whether
\underline{u}
is sound relies on counting embeddings that
avoid

u_{i}
.

We now formulate a sequence of steps, that we will prove later, claiming that certain properties hold.
Let
X_{2}(v_{1})\subseteq N_{\Gamma}(v_{1};V^{2})
be the set of vertices
u_{2}\in N_{\Gamma}(v_{1};V^{2})
such that
(v_{1},u_{2})
is sound and
\deg_{\Gamma}(v_{1},u_{2};V^{3})\geq\tfrac{d^{2}n}{2}
.

Step 1
.

With probability at least
1-n^{-6}
, the following statement holds in
\Gamma_{p}
.

If
(v_{1})
is sound, then
|X_{2}(v_{1})|\geq\frac{dn}{2}
.

Given
v_{2}\in V^{2}
, let
X_{3}(v_{1},v_{2})\subseteq N_{\Gamma}(v_{1},v_{2};V^{3})
be the set of vertices
u_{3}\in N_{\Gamma}(v_{1},v_{2};V^{3})
such that
(v_{1},v_{2},u_{3})
is sound. Furthermore, let
Y_{3}(v_{1},v_{2})\subseteq X_{3}(v_{1},v_{2})
be the set of those
u_{3}
such that
v_{2}u_{3}\in E(\Gamma_{p})
.

Step 2
.

With probability at least
1-n^{-6}
the following statement holds in
\Gamma_{p}
for every
v_{2}\in V^{2}
.

If
(v_{1})
is sound and
v_{2}\in X_{2}(v_{1})
, then we have
\left|Y_{3}(v_{1},v_{2})\right|\geq\frac{pd^{2}n}{8}
.

Let now

Z^{\prime}(v_{1})=\left\{(u_{2},u_{3})\in V^{2}\times V^{3}:u_{2}\in X_{2}(v_{1}),u_{3}\in Y_{3}(v_{1},u_{2})\right\}

and

Z(v_{1})=\{(u_{2},u_{3})\in Z^{\prime}(v_{1}):\{v_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}\text{ is a triangle in }\Gamma_{p}\}=\tr_{v_{1}}(\Gamma_{p})\cap Z^{\prime}(v_{1}).

We will use
1
and
2
to deduce the following.

Step 3
.

With probability at least
1-n^{-5}
, the following statement holds in
\Gamma_{p}
.

If
(v_{1})
is sound, then
|Z^{\prime}(v_{1})|\geq\frac{pd^{3}n^{2}}{16}
.

The claim in the following last step will be a consequence of
Lemma

4.6
.

Step 4
.

With probability at least
1-n^{-5}
, the following statement holds in
\Gamma_{p}
.

If
|Z^{\prime}(v_{1})|\geq\frac{pd^{3}n^{2}}{16}
, then we have
|Z(v_{1})|\geq\frac{(pd)^{3}n^{2}}{32}
.

Before we prove the claims in
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
, let us use them to deduce
5.3
. Note that assuming the statements in
3
and
4
hold in
\Gamma_{p}
we have with probability at least
1-2n^{-5}
that if
(v_{1})
is sound then
|Z(v_{1})|\geq\tfrac{(pd)^{3}n^{2}}{32}
. Furthermore,
by the definition of
Z^{\prime}_{1}(v_{1})\supseteq Z(v_{1})
and of
X_{3}(v_{1},u_{2})\supseteq Y_{3}(v_{1},u_{2})
we have that
for all
(u_{2},u_{3})\in Z(v_{1})
, the vector
(v_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
is sound, that is,

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1},\hat{u}_{2},\hat{u}_{3}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq 64\alpha 2^{s}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{3}\,.

Therefore, with probability at least
1-2n^{-5}
,

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{v_{1}}^{t+1}(\Gamma_{p})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{(u_{2},u_{3})\in Z(v_{1})}\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1},\hat{u}_{2},\hat{u}_{3}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(pd)^{3}n^{2}}{32}\cdot 64\alpha 2^{s}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{3}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{s+1}\alpha(pd)^{3(t+1)}((n-1)!_{t})(n!_{t+1})^{2},

as required for the claim.
It remains to prove
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
.

Proof of
1
:
For
i=2,3
, let
A_{i}\coloneq N_{\Gamma}(v_{1};V^{i})
. Furthermore, let
A_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq V^{2}
be the set of vertices
u_{2}\in V^{2}
for which
(v_{1},u_{2})
is
sound and let
A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V^{2}
be the set of vertices
u_{2}\in V^{2}
for
which
\deg(v_{1},u_{2};V^{3})\geq\tfrac{d^{2}n}{2}
. Note that
X_{2}(v_{1})=A_{2}\cap A_{2}^{\prime}\cap A_{2}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
(V^{1},V^{i})
is
(\varepsilon,d)
-super-regular, we
have
|A_{i}|\geq(d-\varepsilon)n
for
i=2,3
. Since
(V^{2},V^{3})
is
\varepsilon
-regular,
we have
|A_{2}^{\prime\prime}|\geq(1-\varepsilon)n
by
Lemma

2.8
. Finally,
observe that
(v_{1})
being sound implies that

\begin{split}\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|&\geq 2^{s}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2}\geq 2^{s_{0}}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2}\\
&\geq(1-\eta)^{n}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2}\,.\end{split}

Hence, it follows
from
Lemma

3.4
with
\ell=2
that
with probability at least
1-n^{-6}
, if
(v_{1})
is sound
then for all but at most
\alpha n
vertices
u_{2}\in V^{2}
we have

\begin{split}\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1},\hat{u}_{2}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|&\geq\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\frac{n-t}{n}\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{n}\cdot 2^{s}(pd)^{3t}(n-1)!_{t}(n!_{t})^{2}\\
&\geq 8\sqrt{\alpha}2^{s}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{2}(n!_{t})\,,\end{split}

showing that
(v_{1},u_{2})
is sound.
Thus, we get
|A_{2}^{\prime}|\geq(1-\alpha)n
with probability at least
1-n^{-6}
and
hence

\left|X_{2}(v_{1})\right|=\left|A_{2}\cap A_{2}^{\prime}\cap A_{2}^{\prime\prime}\right|\geq\frac{dn}{2},

as claimed.

Proof of
2
:
Fix some
v_{2}\in V^{2}
.
Let
X_{3}=X_{3}(v_{1},v_{2})
and
Y_{3}=Y_{3}(v_{1},v_{2})\subseteq X_{3}
.
It follows from an application of
Lemma

3.4
with
\ell=3
and
\eta^{\prime}
replacing
\eta
, that the following statement holds in
\Gamma_{p}
with probability at least
1-n^{-8}
.

If
(v_{1})
is sound and
v_{2}\in X_{2}(v_{1})
, then
\left|X_{3}\right|\geq\frac{d^{2}n}{4}
.

Here we used here that
v_{2}\in X_{2}(v_{1})
implies that
\deg_{\Gamma}(v_{1},v_{2};V^{3})\geq\tfrac{d^{2}n}{2}
as well as the fact that
(v_{1},v_{2})
being sound implies that

\left|\Psi_{\hat{v}_{1},\hat{v}_{2}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq 8\sqrt{\alpha}2^{s_{0}}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{2}(n!_{t})\geq(1-\eta^{\prime})^{n}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{2}(n!_{t}),

in order to appeal to
Lemma

3.4
.

Now note that, in order to determine
X_{3}
, we do not need to reveal edges adjacent to
v_{2}
. That is, the random set of vertices
X_{3}
is determined by
(\Gamma_{\hat{v}_{2}})_{p}
. Therefore, by
Lemma

4.6
Item

(i)
we have that with probability at least
1-n^{-8}
the following statement holds in
\Gamma_{p}
.

If
|X_{3}|\geq\frac{d^{2}n}{4}
, then
\left|Y_{3}\right|\geq\frac{pd^{2}n}{8}
.

Therefore with probability at least
1-n^{-7}
, both the above statements hold in
\Gamma_{p}
and so by combining them we have the desired statement of this step for
v_{2}\in V^{2}
.
Taking a union bound over all
v_{2}\in V^{2}
then completes the proof.

Proof of
3
:
This is a simple case of combining
1
and
2
. Indeed with probability at least
1-n^{-5}
both the statements of
1
and
2
hold in
\Gamma_{p}
. Taking this to be the case, if
(v_{1})
is sound, we then have that

|Z^{\prime}(v_{1})|=\sum_{u_{2}\in X_{2}(v_{1})}\left|Y_{3}(v_{1},u_{2})\right|\geq\frac{dn}{2}\cdot\frac{pd^{2}n}{8}=\frac{pd^{3}n^{2}}{16},

as required.

Proof of
4
:
This is a direct application of
Lemma

4.6
Item

(ii)
. Indeed, note that
Z^{\prime}(v_{1})\subseteq\tr_{v_{1}}(\Gamma)
is a random subset of edges determined by
(\Gamma_{\hat{v}_{1}})_{p}
. The conclusion of
4
then follows immediately from

Lemma

4.6
Item

(ii)
.

This concludes the proof of
5.3
and hence the proof of the lemma.

∎

∎

6
Proof of the Local Distribution Lemma

The purpose of this section is to prove the Local Distribution Lemma,
Lemma

3.4
. We will begin by reducing
Lemma

3.4
to another lemma,
Lemma

6.1
below, using a simple averaging argument. Before proving
Lemma

6.1
, we will then take a detour, establishing an Entropy Lemma (
Lemma

6.4
) which will be crucial for the proof of Lemma
6.1
, which is finally given in
Section

6.3
.

6.1
A simplification

Given some
t,\ell
and
\underline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})
as in the statement of Lemma
3.4
, we aim to prove a lower bound on the size of
\Psi^{t}_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}_{\ell}}
for almost all of the
u_{\ell}\in V^{\ell}
. The key step for this is given in the following lemma, which we now motivate.
Given that
\Psi^{t}_{\hat{\underline{u}}}
is large, a simple averaging argument shows that
Eq.

3.6
is true ‘on average’ (i.e. if we take the average of
|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}_{\ell}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})|
over all
u_{\ell}\in V^{\ell}
). That is, there is a vertex
u
such that the assumption on
|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})|
in Lemma
6.1
below holds. Lemma
6.1
then states that this implies that
Eq.

3.6
holds indeed for almost all choices of
u_{\ell}
, which is the challenging part in the proof of Lemma
3.4
. In order to prove Lemma
6.1
in
Section

6.3
, we compare the difference in the sizes of
\Psi^{t}_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}_{\ell}}
for different choices of
u_{\ell}\in V^{\ell}
using the Entropy Lemma (
Lemma

6.4
).

Lemma 6.1
.

For all
0<\alpha,d\leq 1
and
K>0
there exists
\eta,\varepsilon>0
and
C>0
such that for all sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
p\geq C(\log n)^{1/3}n^{-2/3}
, if
\,\Gamma
is an
(\varepsilon,d)
-super-regular tripartite graph with parts of size
n
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
,
\ell\in[3]
,
\underline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
and
u\in V^{\ell}
then the following holds in
\Gamma_{p}
with probability at least
1-n^{-K}
.
If

\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq(1-\eta)^{n}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{\ell}(n!_{t})^{3-\ell},

then

\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{v}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\cdot\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|

for at least
(1-\alpha)n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
.

Indeed, with
Lemma

6.1
in hand,
Lemma

3.4
follows easily.

Proof of
Lemma

3.4
.

Fix
\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{C}\ll\eta\ll d,\alpha
. Fix
\Gamma
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
with
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
,
\ell\in[3]
and
\underline{u}=(u_{1}\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
.
By applying
Lemma

6.1
with
K+1
replacing
K
and taking a union bound, we have that with probability at least
1-n^{-K}
, the conclusion of
Lemma

6.1
holds in
G=\Gamma_{p}
for all
u\in V^{\ell}
. So suppose that this is the case and further suppose that

\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{t}(G)\right|\geq(1-\eta)^{n}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{\ell-1}(n!_{t})^{4-\ell}.

Now, for each
\psi\in\Psi_{\hat{\underline{u}}}^{t}(G)
, we have
\psi\in\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}_{\ell}}^{t}(G)
for exactly
n-t
choices of
u_{\ell}\in V^{\ell}
. Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in V^{\ell}}\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\right|

\displaystyle=(n-t)\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{t}(G)\right|.

By averaging, there must be some
u^{*}\in V^{\ell}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}^{*}}^{t}(G)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{n-t}{n}\right)\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{t}(G)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{n-t}{n}\right)(1-\eta)^{n}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{\ell-1}(n!_{t})^{4-\ell}

\displaystyle=(1-\eta)^{n}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{\ell}(n!_{t})^{3-\ell}.

The result now follows from applying the assumed conclusion of
Lemma

6.1
with
u^{*}
playing the rôle of
u
.
∎

6.2
The Entropy Lemma

In this section, we will prove a key lemma,
Lemma

6.4
, which we call the Entropy Lemma. We start with some definitions.
Given some tripartite
\Gamma
with parts of size
n
, some
\ell\in[3]
,
t\in[n]
and some
\psi\in\Psi^{t}(\Gamma)
, we define
I^{\ell}(\psi)\subset V^{\ell}
to be the vertices in
V^{\ell}
which are isolated in the embedded subgraph
\psi(D_{t})
. If
\ell
is clear from context, we will drop the superscript.
If we are further given some
v\in V^{\ell}
, we define

\psi_{v}=\begin{cases}\hfil\emptyset&\text{if }v\in I(\psi),\\
\left(N_{\psi(D_{t})}\left(v;V^{j}\right):j\in J\right)&\text{if }v\not\in I(\psi),\end{cases}

where
J=[3]\setminus\{\ell\}
.
So
\psi_{v}
either returns an empty set, indicating that the vertex
v
is isolated in
\psi(D_{t})
, or it returns the pair of vertices which are contained in the triangle containing
v
in
\psi(D_{t})
.
We also define the function

Y_{v}(\psi)=\mathbbm{1}[\{\psi_{v}\neq\emptyset\}]=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\psi_{v}\neq\emptyset,\\
0&\text{if }\psi_{v}=\emptyset,\end{cases}

which returns
1
if
v\not\in I(\psi)
and
0
otherwise.
Note that for any
\ell\in[3]
the set
\{\psi_{v}:v\in V^{\ell}\}
completely determines the (unordered) subgraph
\psi(D_{t})
.

For a fixed
u\in V^{\ell}
and
v\in V^{\ell}\setminus\{u\}
, we will be interested in the distribution of
\psi^{*}_{v}
if
\psi^{*}
is chosen randomly among a set of embeddings we wish to extend.
In order to analyse this, we use entropy. See
Section

2.5
for the definition and basic properties.
We remark that there will be two independent stages of randomness in the argument.
First, there is the random subgraph
\Gamma_{p}\subseteq\Gamma
, and second, there will be a randomly chosen
\psi^{*}\in\Psi^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
.
In particular, the values of the entropy function
h(\psi^{*}),h(\psi^{*}_{v})
are random variables themselves. However, once we fix a particular instance
G=\Gamma_{p}
, these values are deterministic.
We proceed with the following definition which will be convenient to ease notation in what follows.

Definition 6.2
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
p=p(n)\in(0,1)
and
0<d\leq 1
, we define

H=H(n,p,d)\coloneq\log\left((pd)^{3}\cdot n^{2}\right).

To see the relevance of this function, note that in a random sparsification of the complete tripartite graph
K_{n,n,n}
with probability
pd
, we would expect a given vertex to lie in
(pd)^{3}n^{2}
triangles. Therefore if we fix a vertex
v
and take a uniformly random triangle containing
v
, we expect the entropy of the random variable which chooses this triangle, to be roughly
H(n,p,d)
. The function
H
can thus be seen as benchmark for the maximum entropy (recalling
Lemma

2.13
) of a randomly chosen triangle containing a fixed vertex. Our aim will be to show that, for most choices of fixed vertex
v
,
H
is a good approximation for the entropy of the random variable
\psi^{*}_{v}
discussed above.

We begin with observing that the function
H
provides an appropriate upper bound on the entropy we will be interested in.

Observation 6.3
.

For all
0<\varepsilon^{\prime}<d\leq 1
and
L>0
there exists
\varepsilon>0
and
C>0
such that for all sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
p\geq C(\log n)^{1/3}n^{-2/3}
, if
\Gamma
is an
(\varepsilon,d)
-super-regular tripartite graph with parts of size
n
,
t\in[n]
,
\ell\in[3]
,
\underline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
and
u\in V^{\ell}
then the following holds in
\Gamma_{p}
with probability at least
1-n^{-L}
.

For
\psi^{*}
chosen uniformly from
\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
, we have that

h(\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1)\leq H(n,p,d)+\varepsilon^{\prime}
for all but at most
\varepsilon^{\prime}n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
.

Proof.

Choose
0<\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{C}\ll\varepsilon^{\prime},d,\tfrac{1}{L}
. By
4.4
, we have that with probability at least
1-n^{-L}
,

\left|\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{p})\right|=(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})(pd)^{3}n^{2},

for all but at most
\varepsilon^{\prime}n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
.
In particular, for each such
v
, we have
\log\left|\tr_{v}((\Gamma_{p})_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}})\right|\leq H(n,p,d)+\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

2.13
, we have
h(\psi_{v}^{*}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1)\leq H(n,p,d)+\varepsilon^{\prime}
for all
v
as above and for
\psi^{*}\in\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
chosen uniformly at random.
∎

The main purpose of this section is to provide a partial converse to the above observation, showing that for almost all vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
,
H
is a good approximation for the entropy
h(\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1)
. The full statement is as follows.

Lemma 6.4

(Entropy Lemma)
.

For all
0<\beta,d\leq 1
and
L>0
there exists
\eta,\varepsilon>0
and
C>0
such that for all sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
p\geq C(\log n)^{1/3}n^{-2/3}
, if
\,\Gamma
is an
(\varepsilon,d)
-super-regular tripartite graph with parts of size
n
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\left(1-\eta\right)n\leq t<n
,
\ell\in[3]
,
\underline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
and
u\in V^{\ell}
then the following holds in
\Gamma_{p}
with probability at least
1-n^{-L}
. If

\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|\geq(1-\eta)^{n}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{\ell}(n!_{t})^{3-\ell},

and
\psi^{*}
is chosen uniformly from
\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
, then we have that
h(\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1)\geq H(n,p,d)-\beta
for all but at most
\beta n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
.

In the remainder of this section, we will prove
Lemma

6.4
. Recall that we have
V(\Gamma)=V(\Gamma_{p})=V^{1}\cup V^{2}\cup V^{3}
with each
V^{i}
of size
n
.
As above, for
t\in[n]
, an embedding
\psi\in\Psi^{t}(\Gamma)
and some
\ell\in[3]
, we denote by
I(\psi)=I^{\ell}(\psi)
the vertices in
V^{\ell}
which are not contained in the subgraph
\psi(D_{t})
.
In the proof, we will describe
\psi
by revealing the status of
\psi_{v}
one by one for each
v\in V^{\ell}
according to some linear order
\sigma
of
V^{\ell}
. In order to do so, we need to make some further definitions. Firstly we denote by
w<_{\sigma}v
that
w
occurs before
v
in the ordering
\sigma
.
Now given some fixed
t
,
\psi
and
\ell
as above and an ordering
\sigma
of
V^{\ell}
, we will be interested in revealing
\psi\in\Psi^{t}(\Gamma)
according to the ordering
\sigma
as follows. We imagine processing the vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
in order and as we process each vertex
v
we reveal its status in
\psi
by revealing
\psi_{v}
. Either
v
is not in a triangle in
\psi(D_{t})
or
v
is in a triangle, in which case, we are given the other vertices of the triangle containing
v
in
\psi(D_{t})
. Now consider the moment before processing some vertex
v\in V^{\ell}
. At this point, we know all the triangles in
\psi(D_{t})
that contain vertices
w\in V^{\ell}
such that
w<_{\sigma}v
. We are interested in which vertices are candidates to feature in
\psi_{v}
at this point and the following definition captures this.

For some fixed
t
,
\psi
and
\ell
as above, an ordering
\sigma
of
V^{\ell}
, some
\underline{u}\in\mathcal{V}
, some
j\in[3]\setminus\{\ell\}
and some
v\in V^{\ell}
we define

A_{v}^{j}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\coloneq\left\{a\in V^{j}_{\hat{\underline{u}}}:a\not\in\bigcup_{w\in V^{\ell}:\ w<_{\sigma}v}\psi_{w}\right\}

and
A_{v}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\coloneq\bigcup_{j\in J}A_{v}^{j}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})
, where
J\coloneq[3]\setminus\{\ell\}
.
We think of these vertices as being
‘alive’
at the point just before processing
v
(when we are about to reveal
\psi_{v}
).
By ‘alive’, we mean that it is still possible that
\psi_{v}
reveals that
a\in A_{v}^{j}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})
is in a triangle with
v
. All other vertices
a\in V^{j}\setminus A^{j}_{v}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})
are already embedded in triangles with vertices
w\in V^{\ell}
which come before
v
in the ordering
\sigma
(or lie in
\underline{u}
in which case we are forbidden from including them in a triangle in
\psi
).

Triangles with alive vertices

In this subsection, we will prove that most vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
are in the expected number of triangles with the other two vertices still being ‘alive’.
This will be useful in the proof of the Entropy Lemma,
Lemma

6.4
.

Lemma 6.5
.

For all
0<\tau<d\leq 1
and
L>0
there exists
\varepsilon>0
and
C>0
such that for all sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
p\geq C(\log n)^{1/3}n^{-2/3}
, if
\,\Gamma
is an
(\varepsilon,d)
-regular tripartite graph with parts of size
n
then the following holds in
\Gamma_{p}
with probability at least
1-n^{-L}
. If
t\in[n-1]
,
\ell\in[3]
,
\underline{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
,
u\in V^{\ell}
,

\psi\in\Psi_{{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
and
\sigma
is an ordering of
V^{\ell}
, then there are at most
\tau n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
for which

\left|\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{p})\cap E(\Gamma[A_{v}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})])\right|>(pd)^{3}\prod_{j\in J}\left|A^{j}_{v}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\right|+\tau(pd)^{3}n^{2},

(6.1)

where, as above,
J=[3]\setminus\{\ell\}
.

Proof.

Choose
0<\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{C}\ll\varepsilon^{\prime}\ll\tau,d,\tfrac{1}{L}
. Let
G\subseteq\Gamma
be any subgraph satisfying

\left|K_{3}(G[X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup X_{3}])\right|\leq(pd)^{3}|X_{1}||X_{2}||X_{3}|+\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{3}n^{3},

(6.2)

for all
X_{1}\subseteq V^{1}
,
X_{2}\subseteq V^{2}
,
X_{3}\subseteq V^{3}
and note that
\Gamma_{p}
is such a subgraph with probability at least
1-n^{-L}
by
Lemma

4.1
.
We will show that
G
already satisfies the conclusion of
Lemma

6.5
.
Let
\ell\in[3]
,
t\in[n-1]
,
\underline{u}=(u_{1}\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
,
u_{\ell}\in V^{\ell}
,
\psi\in\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)
and let
\sigma
be an ordering of
V^{\ell}
.
Enumerate
V^{\ell}=\{v_{1}^{\ell},\ldots,v_{n}^{\ell}\}
according to the ordering
\sigma
, that is, in such a way that
v_{1}^{\ell}<_{\sigma}\cdots<_{\sigma}v_{n}^{\ell}
.
Define
U\subseteq V^{\ell}
to be the set of vertices satisfying
Eq.

6.1
. We will show that
|U|<\tau n
.
We split
V^{\ell}
into intervals as follows. Let
\tau^{\prime}:=\tfrac{\tau}{4}
,
K:=\lceil\tfrac{1}{\tau^{\prime}}\rceil
and for
k=1,\ldots,K
, let

W_{k}=\{v^{\ell}_{i}:1+(k-1)\cdot\tau^{\prime}n\leq i<1+k\cdot\tau^{\prime}n\}

and
U_{k}\coloneq U\cap W_{k}
.
Fix some
k\in[K]
and let
i_{k}\coloneq 1+\lceil(k-1)\cdot\tau^{\prime}n\rceil
and
w_{k}\coloneq v^{\ell}_{i_{k}}
(that is,
w_{k}
is the first vertex in
W_{k}
). Let
X_{\ell}=U_{k}
and
X_{j}=A_{w_{k}}^{j}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})
for
j\in J=[3]\setminus\{\ell\}
.
It follows that, for any
z\in U_{k}
,

\displaystyle\left|\tr_{z}(G[\cup_{i\in[3]}X_{i}])\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\tr_{z}(G[X_{\ell}\cup A_{z}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})])\right|

\displaystyle\geq(pd)^{3}\prod_{j\in J}\left|A^{j}_{z}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\right|+\tau(pd)^{3}n^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(pd)^{3}\prod_{j\in J}\left(\left|X_{j}\right|-\tau^{\prime}n\right)+\tau(pd)^{3}n^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(pd)^{3}\prod_{j\in J}\left|X_{j}\right|+\frac{\tau}{2}(pd)^{3}n^{2}.

Here, the first inequality follows from the fact that
z>_{\sigma}w_{k}
and thus
A_{z}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\subseteq A_{w_{k}}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})
for every
z\in U_{k}
.
The second inequality follows from the fact that
z\in U
and the third from the fact that
\left|A_{z}^{j}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\right|\geq\left|A_{w_{k}}^{j}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\right|-\tau^{\prime}n
for all
z\in U_{k}
since
z
and
w_{k}
are close in the ordering
\sigma
.
By summing over all
z\in U_{k}
, it follows that

\left|K_{3}(G[X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup X_{3}])\right|\geq(pd)^{3}\left|X_{1}\right|\left|X_{2}\right|\left|X_{3}\right|+\frac{\tau}{2}(pd)^{3}|X_{\ell}|n^{2}.

Combining this with
Eq.

6.2
gives
|U_{k}|=|X_{\ell}|\leq\frac{2\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\tau d^{3}}n<\frac{\tau^{2}}{8}n
, by our choice of constants.
It follows that
|U|=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left|U_{k}\right|<\tau n
, as claimed.
∎

Proof of the Entropy Lemma

Here, we will prove
Lemma

6.4
. The proof is quite long and so we will break it up into smaller claims along the way. Our proof works by contradiction. As
\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})\right|
is large, we know that
h(\psi^{*})
is large as
\psi^{*}
is chosen uniformly at random from
\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
. Moreover, using the chain rule (Lemma
2.17
), we can decompose
h(\psi^{*})
as the sum of local entropy values depending on the
\psi_{v}^{*}
. Now we assume that there are a significant number of bad vertices
v
for which the local entropy value
h(\psi_{v}^{*}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1)
is too small. We will then apply the chain rule (Lemma
2.17
) using an ordering on the vertices which places these bad vertices at the beginning of the ordering. This has the effect that the shortcoming of their contribution to the overall entropy
h(\psi^{*})
is felt the most. We then upper bound the contribution of the entropy values at other (good) vertices, and hence conclude that the overall entropy
h(\psi^{*})
is too small, giving a contradiction. In order to achieve this upper bound, we rely on random properties of
\Gamma_{p}
and we have to split the entropy values further, delving into the average that outputs the entropy values and looking at individual embeddings.

Proof of
Lemma

6.4
.

Choose
0<\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{C}\ll\tau\ll\eta\ll\delta\ll\gamma\ll\beta,d,\tfrac{1}{L}
. Fix
\Gamma
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell\in[3]
,
\underline{u}=(u_{1}\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
, and
u\in V^{\ell}
as in the statement of
Lemma

6.4
.
Assume
G\subseteq\Gamma
is a subgraph of
\Gamma
with
V(G)=V(\Gamma)
which satisfies the following properties
for all
\psi\in\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)
and every ordering
\sigma
of
V^{\ell}
.

(P.1
)

For all vertices
v\in V(G)
, we have

\left|\tr_{v}(G)\right|\leq 10p^{3}n^{2}.

(P.2
)

There are at most
\tau n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
for which

\left|\tr_{v}(G)\cap E(G[A_{v}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})])\right|>(pd)^{3}\prod_{j\in[3]\setminus\{\ell\}}\left|A^{j}_{v}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\right|+\tau(pd)^{3}n^{2}.

By Lemmas
6.5
,
4.5
and a union bound,
\Gamma_{p}
satisfies these properties with probability at least
1-n^{-L}
and therefore it suffices to show that any
G
satisfying the above properties, satisfies the conclusion of
Lemma

6.4
.

To ease notation, let
\Psi\coloneq\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)
. Furthermore, let
\psi^{*}
be chosen uniformly from
\Psi
.
We may assume that

\left|\Psi\right|\geq(1-\eta)^{n}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{\ell}(n!_{t})^{3-\ell},

as otherwise there is nothing to prove. In particular, by
Lemma

2.13
, we have

\displaystyle h(\psi^{*})

\displaystyle\geq n\log(1-\eta)+3t\log(pd)+3\log(n!_{t})-3\log(n)

\displaystyle\geq 3t\log(pd)+3\log(n!_{t})-\delta n,

(6.3)

where we used
\eta\ll\delta
and that
n
is large enough in the last step.

Assume for a contradiction that there are at least
\beta n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
such that
h(\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1)<H(n,p,d)-\beta
and let
U\subset V^{\ell}
be a set of these exceptional vertices of size
|U|=\gamma n
. We will derive an upper bound on
h(\psi^{*})
which contradicts
Eq.

6.3
.
Recall that
I(\psi)=I^{\ell}(\psi)\subset V^{\ell}
is the set of vertices which are isolated in
\psi(D_{t})
. We begin as follows

\displaystyle h(\psi^{*})

\displaystyle=h\left(\psi^{*},\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}},I(\psi^{*})\right)

(6.4)

\displaystyle=h\left(\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}},I(\psi^{*})\right)+h\left(\psi^{*}|\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}},I(\psi^{*})\right)

(6.5)

\displaystyle\leq h\left(\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}},I(\psi^{*})\right)+\log(t!)

(6.6)

\displaystyle=h\left(\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}}|I(\psi^{*})\right)+h\left(I(\psi^{*})\right)+\log(t!)

(6.7)

\displaystyle\leq h\left(\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}}|I(\psi^{*})\right)+\log(t!)+\log\left(\binom{n}{t}\right)

(6.8)

\displaystyle=h\left(\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}}|I(\psi^{*})\right)+\log(n!_{t}).

(6.9)

Here, we used
Lemma

2.15
in
Eq.

6.4
and the chain rule (
Lemma

2.17
) in
Eq.

6.5
and
Eq.

6.7
.
In
Eq.

6.6
, we used
Lemma

2.18
coupled with the fact that the set
\{\psi_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}}
completely determines the (unordered) subgraph
\psi(D_{t})
. Indeed, note that there are
t!
embeddings
\psi\in\Psi
which map to the same subgraph
\psi(D_{t})
, namely one for each choice of ordering of the triangles.
Finally, in
Eq.

6.8
we used
Lemma

2.13
.

Now, in order to estimate this sum further, we fix some ordering
\sigma
of
V^{\ell}
in which the vertices in
U
come first, that is
w<_{\sigma}w^{\prime}
for all
w\in U
and
w^{\prime}\in V^{\ell}\setminus U
. We then reveal vertices in that order and apply the conditional chain rule (
Lemma

2.20
). That is,

\displaystyle h\left(\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}}|I(\psi^{*})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V^{\ell}}h\left(\psi^{*}_{v}|\{\psi^{*}_{w}:w<_{\sigma}v\},I(\psi^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v\in U}h\left(\psi^{*}_{v}|I(\psi^{*})\right)+\sum_{v\in V^{\ell}\setminus U}h\left(\psi^{*}_{v}|\{\psi^{*}_{w}:w<_{\sigma}v\},I(\psi^{*})\right),

(6.10)

where we applied
Lemma

2.16
in the second step. We treat the vertices in
U
separately to those in
V^{\ell}\setminus U
. To ease notation, we make the following definition. For
\psi\in\Psi
and
v\in V^{\ell}
, we let
t_{v}(\psi)
denote the number of vertices
w\in V^{\ell}
such that
w<_{\sigma}v
and
w\notin I(\psi)
. Let us first address the vertices in
U
.

Claim 6.6
.

For all
v\in U
, we have that

h(\psi^{*}_{v}|I(\psi^{*}))\leq\frac{1}{\left|\Psi\right|}\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}Y_{v}(\psi)\left(\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)-\frac{\beta}{2}\right).

Proof of Claim
.

Now, for each
v\in U
, we have

\displaystyle h(\psi^{*}_{v}|I(\psi^{*}))

\displaystyle\leq h(\psi_{v}^{*}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*}))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1\right]h(\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1)+\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=0\right]h(\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=0)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1\right]\left(H(n,p,d)-\beta\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|\Psi\right|}\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}Y_{v}(\psi)\left(H(n,p,d)-\beta\right).

Here we used
Lemma

2.16
and the fact that
I(\psi^{*})
determines
Y_{v}(\psi^{*})
, the definition of conditional entropy
Eq.

2.2
, and the definition of
U
.
Furthermore, we have
t_{v}(\psi)\leq\gamma n
for all
v\in U
and
\psi\in\Psi
since
U
comes at the beginning of the ordering
\sigma
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\log\left((pd)^{3}(1-\gamma)^{2}n^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=H(n,p,d)+2\log(1-\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq H(n,p,d)-4\gamma

\displaystyle\geq H(n,p,d)-\frac{\beta}{2}.

Combining this with our upper bound on
h(\psi^{*}_{v}|I(\psi^{*}))
above completes the proof of the claim.

∎

We will now deal with the vertices outside
U
. Given
v\in V^{\ell}
and
\psi\in\Psi
, we write

h^{\prime}(v,\psi)\coloneq h\left(\psi_{v}^{*}|I(\psi^{*})=I(\psi),\{\psi^{*}_{w}=\psi_{w}\}_{w<_{\sigma}v}\right).

Claim 6.7
.

The following is true for all
\psi\in\Psi
.

(i)

For all
v\in V^{\ell}
, we have

h^{\prime}(v,\psi)\leq\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)+\log\left(\frac{10}{d^{3}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}}\right).

(ii)

There exists a set
B(\psi)\subset V^{\ell}
with
|B(\psi)|\leq\delta n
, such that for all
v\in V^{\ell}\setminus B(\psi)
, we have

h^{\prime}(v,\psi)\leq\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)+\delta.

Proof of Claim
.

The first inequality follows from
Item

(P.1)
and
Lemma

2.18
. Indeed, for all
v\in V^{\ell}
, we have

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(v,\psi)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(\left|\tr_{v}(G)\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log(10p^{3}n^{2})

\displaystyle=\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)+\log\left(\frac{10}{d^{3}}\right)+\log\left(\frac{n^{2}}{(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}}\right).

For the second inequality, we will use
Item

(P.2)
in combination with
Lemma

2.18
. We have that for all but at most
\tau n
vertices,

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(v,\psi)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(\left|\tr_{v}(G)\cap E(G[A_{v}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})])\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left((pd)^{3}\prod_{j\in J}\left|A^{j}_{v}(\psi,\sigma,\underline{u})\right|+\tau(pd)^{3}n^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}+\tau(pd)^{3}n^{2}\right).

(6.11)

Observe that
t_{v}(\psi)\leq\big(1-\tfrac{\delta}{2}\big)n
for all but at most
\tfrac{\delta n}{2}
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
. In particular, we have

(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\geq\frac{\delta^{2}n^{2}}{4}\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4\tau}\cdot\tau n^{2}\geq\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot\tau n^{2},

for all but at most
\tfrac{\delta n}{2}
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
(we used that
\tau\ll\delta
here).
Plugging this back into
Eq.

6.11
, we get

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(v,\psi)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left((1+\delta)\cdot(pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)\leq\delta+\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)

for all but at most
\big(\tau+\tfrac{\delta}{2}\big)n\leq\delta n
vertices
v\in V^{\ell}
.

∎

We will now use
6.6
and
6.7
to finish the proof.
Indeed, it follows from
6.6
that

\sum_{v\in U}h(\psi^{*}_{v}|I(\psi^{*}))\leq\frac{1}{\left|\Psi\right|}\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\sum_{v\in U}Y_{v}(\psi)\left(\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)-\frac{\beta}{2}\right).

(6.12)

Furthermore, using
6.7
, the definition of conditional entropy
Eq.

2.3
(and
Lemma

2.18
to conclude that
h^{\prime}(v,\psi)=0
if
Y_{v}(\psi)=0
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V^{\ell}\setminus U}

\displaystyle h\left(\psi^{*}_{v}|\{\psi^{*}_{w}:w<_{\sigma}v\},I(\psi^{*})\right)=\sum_{v\in V^{\ell}\setminus U}\frac{1}{\left|\Psi\right|}\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}Y_{v}(\psi)h^{\prime}(v,\psi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\left|\Psi\right|}\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\left(\delta n+N_{1}(\psi)+\sum_{v\in V^{\ell}\setminus U}Y_{v}(\psi)\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)\right),

(6.13)

where

N_{1}(\psi)=\sum_{v\in B(\psi)}Y_{v}(\psi)\left(\log\left(\frac{10}{d^{3}}\right)+2\log\left(\frac{n}{n-t_{v}(\psi)}\right)\right).

Let now

M(\psi)\coloneq\sum_{v\in V^{\ell}}Y_{v}(\psi)\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad N_{2}(\psi)\coloneq\sum_{v\in U}Y_{v}(\psi)\cdot\frac{\beta}{2}.

Then, combining
Eqs.

6.12
,
6.13
and
6.10
, we get

h\left(\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}}|I(\psi^{*})\right)\leq\frac{1}{\left|\Psi\right|}\sum_{\psi\in\Psi}\left(M(\psi)+N_{1}(\psi)+\delta n-N_{2}(\psi)\right).

(6.14)

We will bound each of these terms one by one.

Claim 6.8
.

For all
\psi\in\Psi
, we have that

M(\psi)=3t\log(pd)+2\log(n!_{t}),\qquad N_{1}(\psi)\leq\sqrt{\delta}n\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad N_{2}(\psi)\geq\gamma^{2}n.

Before we prove
6.8
, let us finish the main proof. Combining
6.8
with
Eq.

6.14
, we get (using
\delta\ll\gamma
) that

\displaystyle h\left(\{\psi^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\ell}}|I(\psi^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\leq 3t\log(pd)+2\log(n!_{t})+(\delta+\sqrt{\delta}-\gamma^{2})n

\displaystyle\leq 3t\log(pd)+2\log(n!_{t})-2\delta n.

Plugging this back into
Eq.

6.9
, we get
that
h(\psi^{*})\leq 3t\log(pd)+3\log(n!_{t})-2\delta n,

contradicting
Eq.

6.3
.
Hence it remains to prove
6.8
.

Proof of Claim
.

Let
\psi\in\Psi
and observe that
\{t_{v}(\psi):v\in V^{\ell}\setminus I(\psi)\}=[t-1]_{0}
. Thus

\displaystyle M(\psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V^{\ell}\setminus I(\psi)}\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-t_{v}(\psi))^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\log\left((pd)^{3}(n-k)^{2}\right)=3t\log(pd)+2\log(n!_{t}).

We now turn to bounding
N_{1}(\psi)
. We define
B^{\prime}=:B(\psi)\setminus I(\psi)
and observe that
\left|B^{\prime}\right|\leq\left|B(\psi)\right|\leq\delta n
.
Further, let
\mathds{K}=\{t_{v}(\psi):v\in B^{\prime}\}
. Enumerate
\mathds{K}=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{|B^{\prime}|}\}
so that
k_{1}\geq\ldots\geq k_{|B^{\prime}|}
and observe that
k_{i}\leq n-i
for all
i\in[|B^{\prime}|]
, by virtue of the fact that
t_{v}(\psi)\leq t\leq n-1
for all
v\in B^{\prime}
and, as
B^{\prime}\cap I(\psi)=\emptyset
, we cannot have that
t_{v}(\psi)=t_{v^{\prime}}(\psi)
for
v\neq v^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
. Hence,

\displaystyle N_{1}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in B^{\prime}}Y_{v}(\psi)\left(\log\left(\frac{10}{d^{3}}\right)+2\log\left(\frac{n}{(n-t_{v}(\psi))}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\delta n\log\left(\frac{10}{d^{3}}\right)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\delta n}2\log\left(\frac{n}{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\delta n\log\left(\frac{10}{d^{3}}\right)+2\delta n\log(n)-2\log((\delta n)!)

\displaystyle\leq\delta n\log\left(\frac{10}{d^{3}}\right)+2\delta n\left(\log(n)-\log\left(\frac{\delta n}{e}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\delta}n,

where we used
(\delta n)!\geq\big(\tfrac{\delta n}{e}\big)^{\delta n}
in the second to last line.
Finally, let
U^{\prime}=U\setminus I(\psi)
and observe that, since
\eta\ll\gamma
, we have
\left|U^{\prime}\right|\geq\tfrac{\gamma n}{2}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle N_{2}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in U^{\prime}}\frac{\beta}{2}\geq\gamma^{2}n,

as claimed.

∎

∎

6.3
Counting via comparison

In this subsection, we will prove
Lemma

6.1
which we used in
Section

6.1
to prove the Local Distribution Lemma (
Lemma

3.4
).
Elements of the proof of
Lemma

6.1
were already sketched in
Section

3
but before embarking on the details, we outline and reiterate some of the key ideas, ignoring the technicalities in order to elucidate the general proof scheme. For this discussion, we fix some
(\varepsilon,d)
-super-regular tripartite graph
\Gamma
, fix
\ell=1
and some
t\in[n]
close to
n
. We also fix a vertex
u\in V^{\ell}
which we think of as satisfying the “if” statement in
Lemma

6.1
and some
typical

v\in V^{\ell}
which we aim to show satisfies the conclusion of
Lemma

6.1
. By typical, we mean that
v\in V^{\ell}
satisfies certain conditions that we have shown whp almost all vertices in
V^{\ell}
satisfy. For example, we can assume that
\psi_{v}^{*}
has large entropy, when
\psi^{*}
is a uniformly random embedding in
\Psi_{\hat{u}}^{t}(\Gamma_{p})
, from the Entropy Lemma (
Lemma

6.4
).

Now our aim is to lower bound the number of embeddings
\psi
of
D_{t}
that leave
v
isolated and we concentrate on the subset of embeddings that place
u
in some triangle (as
t
is large we can expect that almost all embeddings do place
u
in a triangle).
Refining further, we will only count embeddings that place
u
in a triangle with an edge that lies in some special set
F\subset E(\Gamma[V^{2},V^{3}])
. To define
F
, we begin by concentrating on edges in
\tr_{u}(\Gamma)\cap\tr_{v}(\Gamma)
. That is, any edge in
F
will form a triangle with
both

u
and
v
. We then take
F
to be the edges in
\tr_{u}(\Gamma)\cap\tr_{v}(\Gamma)
which appear in
\Gamma_{p}
. Note that we do
not
require that for an edge
w_{2}w_{3}\in F
, any of the edges
vw_{i}
or
uw_{i}
with
i=2,3
, lie in
\Gamma_{p}
, just that they lie in
\Gamma
.

To motivate this definition, we consider a multi-stage revealing process. First, we reveal all edges of
\Gamma_{p}
that are
not
adjacent to
u
or
v
. The definition of
F
comes from the fact that at this point in the process, any edge in
F
has the
potential
to lie in
\tr_{u}(\Gamma_{p})
and also
\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{p})
, depending on which random edges are adjacent to the vertices
u
and
v
. Now note that, in particular, if an edge
e=w_{2}w_{3}\in F

does
end up in
\tr_{u}(\Gamma_{p})
, then it will contribute to embeddings that avoid
v
and place
u
in a triangle. We introduce a weight function
\zeta
on
F
(we will in fact define it more generally on
E(\Gamma[V^{2},V^{3}])
) which precisely counts the contribution to our desired lower bound, from embeddings which use the triangle
u\cup e=\{u,w_{2},w_{3}\}
. That is, for all
w_{2}w_{3}\in F
, we have that
\zeta(w_{2}w_{3})
encodes the number of embeddings of
D_{t-1}
(with
t-1
triangles) in
\Gamma
, that avoid
v

and
the vertices
u,w_{2},w_{3}
. Therefore, as we can assume
F
is large (as
v
is typical, using
Lemma

4.7
), our desired conclusion will follow if we can lower bound the
\zeta
values in (some subset of)
F
.

The central idea of the proof is that we
can
lower bound
\zeta
values in
F
by reasoning about embeddings that place
v
in a triangle (and avoid
u
). Indeed, if we consider a uniformly random embedding
\psi^{*}\in\Psi^{t}_{\hat{u}}(\Gamma_{p})
, as
v
is typical, we know from
Lemma

6.4
, that the random variable
\psi_{v}^{*}
, which encodes the triangle containing
v
in
\psi(D_{t})
, has high entropy. Appealing to
Lemma

2.21
then implies that the distribution of
\psi^{*}_{v}
in
\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{p})
is close to uniform and hence for almost all edges
f\in\tr_{v}{(\Gamma_{p})}
, we have that
\mathbb{P}\left[\psi^{*}_{v}=f\right]
is large (in that it is close to the
average). Moreover, we have that
\mathbb{P}\left[\psi^{*}_{v}=f\right]
is directly proportional to
\zeta(f)
by the definition of
\zeta
. Therefore, using
Lemma

4.6
Item

(ii)
(and observing that the
\zeta
values do not depend on random edges adjacent to
u
or
v
), we can see that we must have a significant proportion of the edges in
F
having large
\zeta
values. Indeed, if this were not the case, then it would be very unlikely that almost all edges in
\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{p})
have large
\zeta
values.

We can therefore conclude that there is some subset
F_{L}\subset F
of half the edges in
F
such that
\zeta(f)
is large for all
f\in F_{L}
. Finally, through another application of
Lemma

4.6
Item

(ii)
, we can show that many edges in
F_{L}
end up in
\tr_{u}(\Gamma_{p})
and therefore contribute to the lower bound on the number of embeddings that leave
v
isolated. We now give the full details of the proof.

Proof of
Lemma

6.1
.

Choose
0<\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{C}\ll\varepsilon^{\prime}\ll\eta\ll\beta^{\prime}\ll\beta\ll\tfrac{1}{L}\ll\alpha,d,\tfrac{1}{K}
. Fix
\Gamma
,
p=p(n)
,
\ell\in[3]
,
(1-\eta)n\leq t<n
,
\underline{u}=(u_{1}\ldots,u_{\ell-1})\in\mathcal{V}
and
u\in V^{\ell}
as in the statement of
Lemma

6.1
. We define
J\coloneq[3]\setminus\{\ell\}
and label the indices of
J
by
j_{1},j_{2}\in[3]
so that
J=\{j_{1},j_{2}\}
.

Now for a subgraph
G
of
\Gamma
, we will make some definitions relative to
G
and posit certain properties of
G
. Our proof will then proceed by first proving that any
G
satisfying all the properties, satisfies the desired conclusion of the lemma. After this we will show that whp we can take that
\Gamma_{p}
satisfies all the defined properties, which will complete the proof. Herein, we fix some subgraph
G
of
\Gamma
for the discussion. Our first property comes from the statement of the lemma.

(Q.1
)

We have

\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\right|\geq(1-\eta)^{n}(pd)^{3t}((n-1)!_{t})^{\ell}(n!_{t})^{3-\ell}.

For
v\in V^{\ell}
, we now define the set of edges which lie in
G
and in the common neighbourhood (with respect to
\Gamma
) of both
u
and
v
. In symbols,

F(v)\coloneq\tr_{u}(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})\cap\tr_{v}(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})\cap E(G)\subseteq V_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{j_{1}}\times V_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{j_{2}}.

(6.15)

Note that here (and throughout this proof), for convenience, we will think of edges in
e=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}\in E(\Gamma[V^{j_{1}}\cup V^{j_{2}}])
as
ordered
pairs
(y_{1},y_{2})\in V^{j_{1}}\times V^{j_{2}}
.

Now let
\psi^{*}
be chosen uniformly from
\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)
.
We define the following subsets of
V^{\ell}
, recalling the definition of
H(n,p,d)
from
Definition

6.2
.

\displaystyle Z_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneq\{v\in V^{\ell}:h(\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1)\geq H(n,p,d)-\beta^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle Z_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneq\left\{v\in V^{\ell}:\left|\tr_{v}(G)\right|=(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})(pd)^{3}n^{2}\right\},

\displaystyle Z_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneq\left\{v\in V^{\ell}:\left|F(v)\right|\geq\frac{d^{5}pn^{2}}{4}\right\},

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\coloneq Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}\cap Z_{3}.

Our second property of
G
posits that
Z
is large.

(Q.2
)

If
Item

(Q.1)
holds in
G
then

|Z|\geq(1-\alpha)n.

We now define the weight functions we will be interested in. For
v\in V^{\ell}\setminus\{u\}
and
(w_{1},w_{2})\in V_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{j_{1}}\times V_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{j_{2}}
, define
\zeta_{v}(w_{1},w_{2})
to be
t
times the number of labelled embeddings of
D_{t-1}
into
G_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u},\hat{v}}
in which both
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are isolated vertices. That is,

\zeta_{v}(w_{1},w_{2})\coloneq t\cdot\left|\Psi^{(t-1)}_{\hat{w}_{1},\hat{w}_{2}}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u},\hat{v}}\right)\right|.

(6.16)

For our last property of
G
, we need a further definition.
For
v\in V^{\ell}
, consider
F(v)
as in
Eq.

6.15
. We split
F(v)
in half according to the values of the weight function
\zeta_{v}
. That is we partition
F(v)
into
F_{S}(v)
and
F_{L}(v)
so that
\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})\leq\zeta(z_{1},z_{2})
for all
(y_{1},y_{2})\in F_{S}(v)
and
(z_{1},z_{2})\in F_{L}(v)
, and
\left|F_{S}(v)\right|=\left|F_{L}(v)\right|\pm 1
. Our final property gives that
G
has many triangles containing
u
(resp.
v
) and the edges of
F_{L}(v)
(resp.
F_{S}(v)
).

(Q.3
)

If
v\in Z
, then

\left|F^{\prime}\right|\geq\frac{d^{5}p^{3}n^{2}}{20},

for both
F^{\prime}=F_{L}(v)\cap\tr_{u}(G)
and
F^{\prime}=F_{S}(v)\cap\tr_{v}(G)
.

We now proceed by taking that
G
satisfies
Item

(Q.2)
and
Item

(Q.3)
and showing that it then satisfies the desired conclusion of the lemma. We will do this by proving that if
G
satisfies
Item

(Q.1)
then every
v\in Z
satisfies

\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{v}}^{t}(G)\right|\geq\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\cdot\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\right|,

which in combination with the fact that
G
satisfies
Item

(Q.2)
, gives what is needed. So let us fix some
v\in Z
. We define the following sets of embeddings.

\displaystyle\Psi_{\hat{u}\hat{v}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\cap\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{v}}^{t}(G),

\displaystyle\Psi_{v\hat{u}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\setminus\Psi_{\hat{u}\hat{v}}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\Psi_{u\hat{v}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{v}}^{t}(G)\setminus\Psi_{\hat{u}\hat{v}}.

In words,
\Psi_{\hat{u}\hat{v}}
consists of those embeddings which leave both
u
and
v
isolated whilst embeddings in
\Psi_{v\hat{u}}
leave
u
isolated but have
v
contained in a triangle, and vice versa for
\Psi_{u\hat{v}}
. Clearly, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\Psi_{\hat{u}\hat{v}}\right|+\left|\Psi_{v\hat{u}}\right|,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{v}}^{t}(G)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\Psi_{\hat{u}\hat{v}}\right|+\left|\Psi_{u\hat{v}}\right|.

If
\left|\Psi_{\hat{u}\hat{v}}\right|\geq\big(\tfrac{d}{10}\big)^{2}\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\right|
, we are done and so we may assume that

\left|\Psi_{v\hat{u}}\right|\geq\left(1-\left(\frac{d}{10}\right)^{2}\right)\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\right|.

(6.17)

In what remains, we will compare the sizes of
\Psi_{v\hat{u}}
and
\Psi_{u\hat{v}}
.
Let
\zeta=\zeta_{v}
be the weight function as defined in
Eq.

6.16
. Observe that

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{v\hat{u}}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in\tr_{v}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)}\zeta(y_{1},y_{2}),\text{ and }

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{u\hat{v}}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in\tr_{u}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)}\zeta(y_{1},y_{2}).

Recall that we took
\psi^{*}
to be a uniformly random embedding in
\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)
. Note that
\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1
is a random variable taking values in
S:=\tr_{v}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)
and the distribution of
\psi^{*}_{v}|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})=1
is determined by
\zeta
. That is, for all
(z_{1},z_{2})\in S
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\psi^{*}_{v}=(z_{1},z_{2})|Y_{v}(\psi^{*})\right]=\frac{\zeta(z_{1},z_{2})}{\sum_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in S}\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})}=\frac{\zeta(z_{1},z_{2})}{\left|\Psi_{v\hat{u}}\right|}.

(6.18)

Moreover, as
v\in Z\subseteq Z_{2}
, we have that
\log(\left|S\right|)\leq\log(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})+H(n,p,d)
and therefore, using also that
v\in Z\subseteq Z_{1}
, we can apply
Lemma

2.21
(with
2\beta^{\prime}
replacing
\beta^{\prime}
) to obtain some set
W^{*}\subseteq S=\tr_{v}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)
with the following properties (using (
6.18
) to unpack the conclusions here):

(i)

{\sum}_{(w_{1},w_{2})\in W^{*}}\zeta(w_{1},w_{2})\geq(1-\beta)\left|\Psi_{v\hat{u}}\right|
;

(ii)

There exists some value
\bar{\zeta}
such that for each
(w_{1},w_{2})\in W^{*}
, we have that

\zeta(w_{1},w_{2})=(1\pm\beta)\bar{\zeta};

(iii)

We have
(1-\beta)|S|\leq|W^{*}|\leq|S|
.

Therefore we can estimate the size of
\Psi_{v\hat{u}}
using
Items

(iii)
,
(i)
and
(ii)
in that order, as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{v\hat{u}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{1-\beta}\right)\sum_{(w_{1},w_{2})\in W^{*}}\zeta(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}\right)|W^{*}|\bar{\zeta}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}\right)|S|\bar{\zeta}\leq 2\bar{\zeta}(pd)^{3}n^{2}.

(6.19)

In the last inequality, we used that
|S|=\left|\tr_{v}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)\right|\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})(pd)^{3}n^{2}
since
v\in Z\subseteq Z_{2}
.

We are now going to lower bound
\left|\Psi_{u\hat{v}}\right|
in a similar way. However, the entropy argument above only shows that
\zeta
is ‘well-behaved’ on
S=\tr_{v}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)
but nothing about
\tr_{u}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)
. Using
Item

(Q.3)
though, we can infer though that
\zeta
is ‘well-behaved’ on a large part of
F(v)
, as defined in
Eq.

6.15
. Recall also our definitions of
F_{L}(v)
and
F_{S}(v)
.

Claim 6.9
.

We have
\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})\geq(1-\beta)\bar{\zeta}
for all
(y_{1},y_{2})\in F_{L}(v)
.

Proof of Claim
.

By
Item

(Q.3)
, we have that

\left|\tr_{v}{\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)}\cap F_{S}(v)\right|\geq\frac{d^{5}p^{3}n^{2}}{20},

noting that
\tr_{v}{\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)}\cap F_{S}(v)=\tr_{v}{\left(G\right)}\cap F_{S}(v)
due to the fact that
F_{S}(v)\subset E(\Gamma_{\hat{}\underline{u}})
.
Furthermore, it follows from
Item

(iii)
and the fact that
v\in Z\subseteq Z_{2}
, that

\left|\tr_{v}{\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)}\setminus W^{*}\right|\leq\beta\left|\tr_{v}{\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)}\right|\leq 2\beta(pd)^{3}n^{2}.

Hence, as
\beta\ll d
, we can conclude that
W^{*}\cap F_{S}(v)\neq\emptyset
and so

(1-\beta)\bar{\zeta}\leq\min_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in W^{*}}\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})\leq\max_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in F_{S}(v)}\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})\leq\min_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in F_{L}(v)}\zeta(y_{1},y_{2}),

using
Item

(ii)
in the first inequality.

∎

We now appeal to
Item

(Q.3)
to lower bound the size of
\left|\Psi_{u\hat{v}}\right|
as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{u\hat{v}}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in\tr_{u}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)}\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in\tr_{u}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)\cap F_{L}(v)}\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle\geq(1-\beta)\bar{\zeta}\left|\tr_{u}\left(G_{\hat{}\underline{u}}\right)\cap F_{L}(v)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\bar{\zeta}d^{5}p^{3}n^{2}}{25},

(6.20)

where we used
6.9
.
Putting
Eq.

6.17
,
Eq.

6.19
and
Eq.

6.20
together, we get that

\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{v}}^{t}(G)\right|\geq\left|\Psi_{u\hat{v}}\right|\geq\frac{\bar{\zeta}d^{5}p^{3}n^{2}}{25}\geq\frac{d^{2}}{50}\left|\Psi_{v\hat{u}}\right|\geq\frac{d^{2}}{100}\left|\Psi_{\hat{}\underline{u},\hat{u}}^{t}(G)\right|,

as required.

It remains to verify that for
G=\Gamma_{p}
the statements in
Item

(Q.2)
and
Item

(Q.3)
hold with probability at least
1-n^{-K}
. We start with
Item

(Q.2)
, which follows simply from
4.4
and
Lemmas

6.4
and
4.7
. Indeed, from those results (using that
\tfrac{1}{L}\ll\tfrac{1}{K}
) and a union bound, with probability at least
1-n^{-2K}
, we have that
|Z_{2}|\geq(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})n
,
|Z_{3}|\geq(1-2\varepsilon)n
and if
Item

(Q.1)
holds in
G=\Gamma_{p}
then
|Z_{1}|\geq(1-\beta^{\prime})n
. It then follows easily by our choice of constants that the statement of
Item

(Q.2)
holds in
G=\Gamma_{p}
with probability at least
1-n^{-2K}
.

For
Item

(Q.3)
, we will appeal to
Lemma

4.6
Item

(ii)
. Note that for a fixed
v\in V^{\ell}\setminus\{u\}
the value of
\zeta_{v}(w_{1},w_{2})
for
(w_{1},w_{2})\in V_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{j_{1}}\times V_{\hat{}\underline{u}}^{j_{2}}
does not depend on the random status of any of the edges containing
u
or
v
. Indeed, our definition of
\zeta_{v}
counts only embeddings that leave both
u
and
v
isolated. We also have that the random set of edges
F(v)
, as defined in
Eq.

6.15
, is independent of the random status of any edges adjacent to
u
or
v
. Consequently, in the language of
Lemma

4.6
, we have that the random sets of edges
F_{L}(v)
and
F_{S}(v)
are
determined
by
(\Gamma_{\hat{u}})_{p}
(resp.
(\Gamma_{\hat{v}})_{p}
). Therefore, for a fixed
v\in V^{\ell}
, two applications of
Lemma

4.6
Item

(ii)
(once for
u
and
F_{L}(v)
and once for
v
and
F_{S}(v)
) give that with probability at least
1-n^{-(2K+1)}
, we have that
Item

(Q.3)
holds for
v
. Here we used that
v\in Z\subseteq Z_{3}
implies that
\left|F_{L}(v)\right|,\left|F_{S}(v)\right|\geq\tfrac{d^{5}pn^{2}}{10}
. Taking a union bound over all
v\in V^{\ell}
, we have that
Item

(Q.3)
holds in
G=\Gamma_{p}
for all
v\in V^{\ell}
, with probability at least
1-n^{-2K}
. A final union bound gives that with probability at least
1-n^{-K}
, both
Item

(Q.2)
and
Item

(Q.3)
hold in
G=\Gamma_{p}
which completes the proof.
∎

7
Stability for a fractional version of the Hajnal–Szemerédi theorem

In this section we discuss some fractional variants of the Hajnal–Szemerédi theorem for clique factors (
2.1
with
x=0
). We will use the results here in our proof reducing
1.2
to
3.1
in
Section

9
. The starting point is to relax the notion of a
K_{k}
-factor to that of a
fractional
K_{k}
-factor
. That is, for a graph
G
, a fractional
K_{k}
-factor in
G
is a weighting
\omega:K_{k}(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
\sum_{K\in K_{k}(G,u)}\omega(K)=1
for all
u\in V(G)
. If all cliques
K\in K_{k}(G)
are assigned weights in
\{0,1\}
, we recover the notion of a
K_{k}
-factor and so the definition of a fractional
K_{k}
-factor is more general. However, from an extremal point of view, the same minimum degree condition is needed to force both objects. Indeed, focusing on the case when
n\in k\mathbb{N}
, the Hajnal–Szemerédi theorem (
2.1
with
x=0
) gives that graphs
G
with
n
vertices and minimum degree at least
\big(\tfrac{k-1}{k}\big)n
have
K_{k}
-factors and hence fractional
K_{k}
-factors whilst the same construction proving tightness for
K_{k}
-factors can be used to show tightness for fractional factors, as we now show. Take a graph
G
to be a complete graph with
n\in k\mathbb{N}
vertices with a clique of size
\tfrac{n}{k}+1
removed to leave an independent set of vertices
I
. Therefore
G
has minimum degree
\delta(G)=\big(\tfrac{k-1}{k}\big)n-1
and suppose for a contradiction that
G
has a fractional
K_{k}
-factor given by a weight function
\omega:K_{k}(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Then we have that
\sum_{K\in K_{k}(G,u)}\omega(K)=1
for all
u\in V(G)
and note that for
w\neq w^{\prime}\in I
, we have that
K_{k}(G,w)\cap K_{k}(G,w^{\prime})=\emptyset
as
I
is an independent set. Therefore

\sum_{K\in K_{k}(G)}\omega(K)\geq\sum_{w\in I}\sum_{K\in K_{k}(G,w)}\omega(K)\geq|I|=\frac{n}{k}+1

but we also have that

\sum_{K\in K_{k}(G)}\omega(K)=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{u\in V(G)}\sum_{K\in K_{k}(G,u)}\omega(K)=\frac{n}{k},

a contradiction. The results of this section, which may be of independent interest, will give stability for this phenomenon, showing that if we avoid the construction detailed above (and other similar constructions), by imposing that
\alpha(G)\leq\big(\tfrac{1}{k}-\eta\big)n
for some
\eta>0
, then a weaker minimum degree condition of
\delta(G)\geq\big(\tfrac{k-1}{k}-\gamma\big)n
for some
\gamma=\gamma(\eta)>0
, suffices to force a fractional
K_{k}
-factor.

We will use that the existence of a fractional
K_{k}
-factor can be encoded by a linear program whose dual is a covering linear program which assigns weights to vertices such that every clique is sufficiently ‘covered’. The duality theorem from linear programming will then be used to transfer between the two settings.

Theorem 7.1

(stability for fractional Hajnal–Szemerédi)
.

For every
\eta>0
and
2\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is some
\gamma>0
such that the following is true for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph with
\delta(G)\geq\big(\tfrac{k-1}{k}-\gamma\big)n
and
\alpha(G)<\big(\tfrac{1}{k}-\eta\big)n
.
Then
G
contains a fractional
K_{k}
-factor.

Proof.

We will prove the theorem for
\gamma=\tfrac{\eta}{8^{k}(k!)^{2}}
.
Observe that the existence of a fractional
K_{k}
-factor is the same as saying that the value of the following packing linear program is
\tfrac{n}{k}
. We ask for non-negative real weights on the elements of
K_{k}(G)
with maximum sum, subject to the condition that the total weight on copies of
K_{k}
at any given vertex is at most
1
. The dual of this is the covering linear program in which we place nonnegative weights on the vertices of
G
, and are aiming at minimising their sum, subject to the constraint that the total weight on the vertices of each element of
K_{k}(G)
is at least
1
. The strong duality theorem for linear programs implies that these two linear programs have the same optimal objective function value. So it is enough to show that the latter linear program has optimal objective function value at least
\tfrac{n}{k}
(and thus exactly
\tfrac{n}{k}
), which we do inductively. More precisely, we want to prove the following claim by induction on
k
. We define
z_{2}=3
and inductively
z_{k}=8k^{2}z_{k-1}
for
k\geq 3
.

Claim 7.2
.

Given any
k\geq 2
and
\gamma>0
, suppose that
G
is an
n
-vertex graph with minimum degree at least
\big(\tfrac{k-1}{k}-\gamma\big)n
and no independent set of size
\big(\tfrac{1}{k}-z_{k}\gamma\big)n
. Suppose
c:V(G)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is any weight function such that for each
Q\in K_{k}(G)
we have
\sum_{v\in Q}c(v)\geq 1
. Then
\sum_{v\in V(G)}c(v)\geq\tfrac{n}{k}
.

Proof of Claim
.

It is convenient to let the vertices of
G
be
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
in order of decreasing weight, i.e.
c(v_{i})\geq c(v_{j})
if
i\leq j
. If
\sum_{i\in[n]}c(v_{i})\geq\tfrac{n}{k}
there is nothing to prove, so we can assume the sum is less than
\tfrac{n}{k}
, and hence in particular that
c(v_{n})<\frac{1}{k}
. We next argue that we can assume
c(v_{n})=0
. Indeed, if
c(v_{n})>0
, then we can define a new weight function by
c^{\prime}(v_{i})\coloneq\tfrac{1}{k}+\mu\big(c(v_{i})-\tfrac{1}{k}\big)
for all
i\in[n]
, where
\mu
is chosen so that
c^{\prime}(v_{n})=0
. Here,
\mu>1
because
c(v_{n})<\frac{1}{k}
. Observe that the
v_{i}
remain ordered by weight with this new weight function.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}c^{\prime}(v_{i})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n}{k}+\mu\sum_{i\in[n]}\left(c(v_{i})-\tfrac{1}{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]}c(v_{i})+(\mu-1)\left(\sum\nolimits_{i\in[n]}c(v_{i})-\tfrac{n}{k}\right)<\sum_{i\in[n]}c(v_{i}).

However, for every
Q\in K_{k}(G)
,

\sum_{v\in Q}c^{\prime}(v)=\sum_{v\in Q}\left(\tfrac{1}{k}+\mu\big(c(v)-\tfrac{1}{k}\big)\right)=1+\mu\left(\sum\nolimits_{v\in Q}c(v)-1\right)\geq 1.

Therefore,
c^{\prime}
also satisfies the condition of
7.2
and we thus can assume
c(v_{n})=0
.

We are now in a position to prove the base case
k=2
. Since
v_{n}
has at least
\big(\tfrac{1}{2}-\gamma\big)n
neighbours, and
c(v_{n})=0
, we see that for each
i
such that
v_{i}v_{n}\in E(G)
, we have
c(v_{i})=1
. In particular,
c(v_{i})=1
for each
i\leq\big(\tfrac{1}{2}-\gamma\big)n
. Furthermore, the vertices
\{v_{i}:i\geq\tfrac{n}{2}+2\gamma n\}
do not form an independent set, so there is an edge within this set. At least one endpoint of this edge has weight at least
\tfrac{1}{2}
. As vertices are ordered by weight, this implies that each vertex
v_{i}
with
\tfrac{n}{2}-\gamma n<i<\tfrac{n}{2}+2\gamma n
has weight at least
\tfrac{1}{2}
. Summing, we obtain weight at least
\tfrac{n}{2}
as desired.

Next, we prove the induction step; let
k\geq 3
. We build a copy of
K_{k}
containing
v_{n}
as follows: we take
u_{1}=v_{n}
, and then for each
2\leq i\leq k-2
in succession, we take
u_{i}
to be the common neighbour of
u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}
with smallest weight. From the minimum degree condition, when we choose
u_{i}
there are at least
n\left(1-(i-1)\big(\tfrac{1}{k}+\gamma\big)\right)
common neighbours to choose from; in particular, we always choose a vertex
v_{j}
with
j\geq\tfrac{3n}{k}-(k-3)\gamma n
, and the common neighbourhood of all
k-2
vertices we choose has size at least
\tfrac{2n}{k}-(k-2)\gamma n
. Now consider the last
\big(\tfrac{1}{k}-k(k-1)\gamma\big)n
of these common neighbours. Since
z_{k}\geq k(k-1)
, they do not form an independent set, so contain an edge
u_{k-1}u_{k}
. Since
\sum_{i=1}^{k}c(u_{i})\geq 1
, and
c(u_{1})=0
, one of these vertices has weight at least
\tfrac{1}{k-1}
. In particular,
c(v_{i})\geq\tfrac{1}{k-1}
whenever
i\leq\big(\tfrac{1}{k}+(k-1)^{2}\gamma\big)n
.

Now let
c^{*}\coloneq c(v_{n/k-(k-1)\gamma n})
, and let
G^{\prime}
denote the subgraph of
G
induced by vertices
v_{i}
with
i\geq\big(\tfrac{1}{k}+(k-1)^{2}\gamma\big)n
. If
c^{*}\geq 1
then we have

\sum_{i\in[n]}c(v_{i})\geq\tfrac{n}{k}-(k-1)\gamma n+\tfrac{1}{k-1}\cdot k(k-1)\gamma n>\tfrac{n}{k}

and we are done; so we can assume
c^{*}<1
.
If
Q
is any copy of
K_{k-1}
in
G^{\prime}
, then
Q
has a common neighbourhood in
G
of size at least
\tfrac{n}{k}-(k-1)\gamma n
, and so in particular
Q
extends to a copy of
K_{k}
in
G
by adding a vertex whose weight is at most
c^{*}
. Thus the function
c^{\prime}(u)\coloneq\tfrac{1}{1-c^{*}}c(u)
on
V(G^{\prime})
is a weight function on
V(G^{\prime})
taking values in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and such that
\sum_{u\in Q}c^{\prime}(u)\geq 1
for each
Q\in K_{k-1}(G^{\prime})
. Furthermore every vertex in
G^{\prime}
has at most
\tfrac{n}{k}+\gamma n
non-neighbours in
G
, at most all of which are in
G^{\prime}
, so the minimum degree of
G^{\prime}
is at least
\tfrac{(k-2)n}{k}-((k-1)^{2}+1)\gamma n
. Since
v(G^{\prime})=\tfrac{(k-1)n}{k}-(k-1)^{2}\gamma n
, we have
\delta(G^{\prime})\geq\tfrac{k-2}{k-1}v(G^{\prime})-\gamma^{\prime}v(G^{\prime})
where
\gamma^{\prime}\coloneq 2k^{2}\gamma
. Furthermore
G^{\prime}
has no independent set of size

\tfrac{1}{k}n-z_{k}\gamma n=\tfrac{1}{k}n-4z_{k-1}\gamma^{\prime}n\leq\tfrac{1}{k-1}v(G^{\prime})-z_{k-1}\gamma^{\prime}v(G^{\prime})\,.

We are therefore in a position to apply the induction hypothesis (that is,
7.2
for
k-1
) to
G^{\prime}
, with
\gamma^{\prime}
replacing
\gamma
. We conclude that

\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}c^{\prime}(u)\geq\tfrac{1}{k-1}v(G^{\prime})\geq\frac{\big(1-\tfrac{1}{k}-(k-1)^{2}\gamma\big)n}{k-1}=\big(\tfrac{1}{k}-(k-1)\gamma\big)n

and so

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}c(v_{i})

\displaystyle\geq c^{*}\big(\tfrac{1}{k}-(k-1)\gamma\big)n+\tfrac{1}{k-1}\cdot k(k-1)\gamma n+(1-c^{*})\cdot\big(\tfrac{1}{k}-(k-1)\gamma\big)n
